A CI step then asserts the stamp landed, so a missing mark reads as an unstamped database rather than as a broken guard. · Handoff 25 A.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **The suite's default target is a marked test tenant, and `jaizbankplc` refuses.** `test19` is marked `test` and carries a full credential pair; `jaizbankplc`, `nrs`, `lasg` and `gtb` are marked `client` and now refuse. `cadra19`, `nrs19` and `gtb19` are deliberately left **unmarked** — fail-closed does the work, and marking a database `client` on a guess would be a false record in the one place the system is now trusting. · Handoff 25 A.
- 2026-09-15 · **Moving the suite off a client's register exposed three hidden dependencies on that register, all of them real defects.** `tests/test_locations.py` asked for `/api/v1/locations/1` outright in four tests — a row id is a fact about one database's insert order, not about the API. `test_audit_logs.py` walked the access-report cursor at `limit=3` and asserted more than one page came back, making the proof a function of how many users the tenant happened to have. And `token_scopes()` was asked `"settings:read" not in scopes` when **`admin` is a wildcard in `require_jwt`**, so a credential holding `admin` but not the named scope took the 403 branch against a server correctly answering 200. All three are the same lesson `token_scopes`' own docstring already carried one level along: a per-tenant fact is not a fact about the API. · Handoff 25 A, found by running rather than by reading.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **The residue removal script is dry-run by default, removes only what self-identifies, and stops rather than cascades.** `scripts/remove_test_residue.py`: `CADRA_APPLY=1` is the only way through; only anchored `=like` names count as self-identification (`pytest-%`, `idem-%`, `H23 %` — `like` wraps its value in `%…%` in Odoo, so the first cut's `H23%` was a substring match that would have claimed a real `PUMP-H23-4`, while anchoring it alone silently dropped the 201-asset `idem-*` fixture family the idempotency tests write); and the moment a fixture is attached to something that does not self-identify, the whole group is left alone and printed. **An asset with an approved disposal is never removed even when its name says `pytest-`** — deleting the record is not a reversal, it is the same fabrication one step on, and report 24 already made that call by marking asset 8396. **Custody rows are never removed on their own**: append-only means append-only. A database marked `production` is refused outright. **The dry run MEASURES rather than predicts** — every candidate is unlinked inside a savepoint that is then rolled back, so the counts are of deletions that really succeeded. The first cut predicted them from a model of the schema and the model was backwards: custody is `ondelete='restrict'` and `custody.unlink()` raises outright, so 352 assets classified as removable would have aborted the run on the first one. Measured on `jaizbankplc`: of 3,894 fixture-named assets, **3,779 removable, 115 refused by the rules** (99 carrying custody rows, 6 carrying approved disposals, 10 pointed at by a service request whose FK is `set null` and would have been silently blanked) and 0 refused by the database; of 368 fixture locations, **255 removable, 17 refused by the rules and 96 refused BY THE DATABASE** on a foreign key from `nrs_asset_transfer` that no reading of the model files predicted. **Service requests self-identify by nothing** — `request.request.name` is a sequence, `REQ-000692` — so the script reports them in words and removes none. Owner-run, exactly as `mark_test_fixtures.py` is. · Handoff 25 B.
- 2026-09-15 · **The residue kept growing after report 24 measured it.** Report 24 counted 3,584 fixture assets of 4,056 on 2026-09-15; later the same day, after that report's own verification runs, the register holds **4,164 assets, 3,894 of them fixtures**. The register total is the comparison that does not depend on how the patterns are matched: **+108 assets between measuring the problem and acting on it.** The measurement did not stop the writing, because nothing did until this handoff — which is the argument for the guard in one number. · Handoff 25 B.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **The FM domain gets its own name and its own row; `systemDomain` had no backing field and loses its.** `adapters.ts` mapped `nrs.asset.domain` onto `Asset.systemDomain`, which `AssetDetail` rendered as "System Domain" on IT assets — so a live tenant showed the raw key `it` for every IT asset while the mock filled it with network identifiers like `ABJ-HQ-MST-233` and kept the page looking right in development. Nothing on `nrs.asset` backs a system domain. The field is now `fmDomain`, rendered under its own label for **every** asset rather than only IT ones (the FM domain is a fact about all of them), with the model's label via `useEnumLabel("domain")` rather than the bare key — and the mock carries FM domain values so development and a live tenant render the same shape. Report 22 was right that a label lookup over the OLD name would only have made a wrong field read convincingly; the fix is the name. · Handoff 25 C1, ruled by the Architect.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **`details.allowed` is deprecated in favour of `allowed_values`, and both are published until the next major version.** The handoff named one site; a search found **three** — the FM-domain refusals on both the POST and PATCH category routes, and the logo-prefix refusal on brand settings. Publishing both is the only non-breaking move: a client keying on `allowed_values` gets nothing today, and one keying on `allowed` breaks on a rename. · Handoff 25 C2.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **`workflow_payment_ref` is written, so it is published — the measurement decided it, not a ruling.** The WorkSpace/Workflow approval callback writes it (`myservice_workflow/models/request_request.py:319,386`) and the request form tracks it, so it is not a dead column. It is now in the request serializer under a `workflow` block, together with `workspace_ref`, which was invisible in exactly the same way and is written by the same callback. · Handoff 25 C3.
- 2026-09-15 · **CLOSED: the CI-series guard ruling is not open.** Report 24 §8 carried it forward from report 22; **report 23 §3 had already settled it in practice.** The `odoo`-must-appear-on-the-line requirement is gone, replaced by an allow-list of the two non-Odoo `<major>.0` shapes that occur in first-party CI files (a tag-pinned marketplace action, a runner runtime key anchored so that `ODOO_VERSION: '15.0'` cannot exempt itself by containing the word `version`). The mutation proof now reads `tests/fixtures/retired-gitlab-ci.yml`, the deleted `.gitlab-ci.yml` byte for byte, instead of an invented `ODOO_SERIES_BRANCH` that never existed — and it finds three real offenders in it where the old logic found none. Nothing is outstanding; the backlog item is removed. · Handoff 25 D.
- 2026-09-15 · **A fresh verification pass found eleven defects in work already called finished, four of them fatal to the handoff's own acceptance criteria.** `CADRA_DATABASE_CLASS` was never forwarded into the container by `docker compose run`, so `ensure_database_class()` was **dead code on every real path** and CI's own assertion step would have failed on the first run — the "all four branches measured" table had been measured through `exec -e`, which is not the path CI or a deploy uses. `CADRA_DATABASE_CLASS=test` outranked `CADRA_PROFILE=prod` and silently downgraded an existing `production` mark, and `bootstrap.sh` does `set -a; . ./.env` — so a developer's `.env` copied onto a server could have stamped a client's live database `test`. Gating `token` left four tests authenticating through `creds` with bare `requests`, so `pytest tests/test_auth.py` against a client register ran **green** with the guard never instantiated. And the removal script's cascade model was backwards. **The guard itself was sound; everything wrong was a CLAIM about it** — "there is no way to authenticate around it", "Odoo will happily cascade" — two confident sentences about systems that had not been checked, in a handoff whose whole subject is a control that was trusted without being checked. · Handoff 25 §6.
- 2026-09-15 · **The acceptance criterion "refuses before writing anything" is met for the register and NOT met literally, and the report says so rather than glossing it.** Classifying the database needs a bearer token, and `POST /auth/token` is not a read: `_session_payload` issues a refresh token which `_issue` CREATEs and the request commits. One operational row lands before the refusal prints. Closing it needs an unauthenticated classification route, and `cadra_route` has no public mode — auth is enforced in `cadra_endpoint` for every route without exception — so it belongs to the "nine pre-envelope routes" backlog item rather than to a handoff about test safety. The conftest docstring claiming "minting a token is a read" was false and is corrected. · Handoff 25 A, stated deviation.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **`mark_test_fixtures.py` silently skipped its own first step, and only verifying the database caught it.** `Handoff23 Approver` is ARCHIVED on `jaizbankplc`, and handoff 24's script looked for it with a plain `Client.search(...)` — Odoo's default `active_test` excludes archived records, so the search returned empty, the loop never ran, and the script printed **nothing whatsoever** about the credential while correctly reporting the six disposals. The run looked clean. Report 24 called both halves idempotent; one had never worked on that tenant. Now searched with `active_test=False` (an archived credential's artefacts are still artefacts — `_is_test_credential_user` already searched that way) and it says so when it finds no such client. Re-run and verified: credential flagged, 6 of 6 disposals disclosed. **The third instrument in two handoffs to report success while doing nothing**, after report 23's unregistered test file and report 22's mutation proof asserting on a name that never existed — the shape is always a step that can find nothing and say nothing. · Handoff 25 B, found by verifying an owner-run script instead of reading its output.


## 2026-09-15 — handoff 26 (the last one)

- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **A record created under a test credential says so on its own face, by derivation and never by convention.** The chain of custody was honest because 352 rows DERIVE `source = 'test'` from `myservice.api.client.is_test_credential`; everything else leaned on a name prefix, which is a thing somebody remembers to type, and `request.request` had no convention at all — its name is a sequence, so 460 requests on `jaizbankplc` can never be identified. `cadra.test.fixture.mixin` (`myservice_api/models/test_fixture.py`) generalises the derivation onto **14 models**. It lives in `myservice_api` and not in the modules owning those models, for the reason `asset_custody.py` records: the column belongs to this module, and a lower module reading it could be ordered apart from it by the dependency graph, raising `UndefinedColumn` on every create. · Handoff 26 A.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **The label is derived, unsettable and unclearable — three properties, not one.** `create()` OVERWRITES whatever arrived in `vals`, so a caller can neither declare itself real nor slander a real record; `write()` refuses to move it **unconditionally**, with no superuser branch, because a superuser branch is what an `odoo shell` one-liner uses; and it is in no `_WRITABLE_FIELDS`, with a source guard failing if it is ever added to one. Writing the value the record already holds is a no-op rather than an error, so an ordinary form save still works. The decision comes from the ACTING USER and never from anything the caller sends — a body field saying "this one is a test" would let anybody disguise a real record as an artefact. · Handoff 26 A.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **It is NOT retroactive, and that is stated in the field's own help text, the changelog and the OpenAPI annotation rather than left for a reader to discover.** `create_uid` is not an answer: it says which user, not what the record IS, and it goes stale the moment a credential is re-homed or removed. So `FALSE` does not mean "real data" — it means "created before 19.0.3.9.0, therefore unknown". Consumers are told to treat TRUE as conclusive and FALSE as unknown for anything older. The register began telling the truth about itself on 2026-09-15 and says nothing about the day before, exactly as handoff 24's access record does. · Handoff 26 A.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **The class is closed, not the instance: a creatable model with no self-label fails the suite.** `tests/test_source_guards.py::test_every_model_a_first_party_path_creates_is_classified` enumerates every model any first-party path can create and fails unless it is labelled by the mixin, labelled by its own older mechanism (`nrs.asset.custody.source`), or exempted **with a written reason** — and a second test fails on an exemption whose reason is missing or perfunctory. The labelled set is PARSED out of `test_fixture.py` rather than copied into the guard, because a copied list is a convention and conventions are the subject. The resolver handles the four spellings that matter, including `Client = env[MODEL]` then `Client.create(...)` — verbatim the spelling that blinded report 25's credential guard — and `self._helper().create(...)`, each with a mutation proof. · Handoff 26 A.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **`mail.message` carries the label rather than being exempted as "not register data".** Chatter is the one creatable thing that lands on a record it did not create: `POST /assets/<id>/transfer-custody` posts to the chatter of a REAL asset. Exempting it would leave test-written history on records that are not fixtures at all, which is the shape of hole the mixin exists to close, on the records where it matters most. `author_id` is not the answer — it resolves to the credential's user only while that credential exists and still points at that user, and `myservice.api.client.write` re-homes users. A stamped fact survives; a join does not. · Handoff 26 A.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **A step that names a record and does not find it fails loudly and names what it looked for.** Three instruments in three handoffs reported success while doing nothing, always the same shape: a step that operates on a named thing, finds no such thing, and treats zero as done. A sweep of `scripts/` and every maintenance path found **24 distinct sites**. The worst was in the newest file: `scripts/remove_test_residue.py` read the production refusal — the one guard between `CADRA_APPLY=1` and a real client's register — through the ormcached `get_param`, the exact call the handoff that WROTE that script had just finished proving unsafe. Thirteen were fixed; the remaining eleven are recorded with file and line in `docs/GO-LIVE.md` §10.2 rather than carried as folklore. Where a zero result is legitimate it now says so deliberately in the code, and `active_test=False` is used wherever the question is "does this record exist" rather than "is this record in use". · Handoff 26 B.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **Both fixture scripts refuse an unclassified database, not only a `production` one.** They write to, and delete from, an asset register. An unmarked database is not a safe one — it is one nobody has classified, and all three of `cadra19`, `nrs19` and `gtb19` are unmarked. `client` is allowed on purpose: `jaizbankplc` is marked `client` and is the register both scripts were written for. · Handoff 26 B.
- 2026-09-15 · `[landed]` **The fixtures that cannot be removed are marked on their NAME, and the mark is a weaker claim than the field, said so explicitly.** Append-only is the property the custody chain is worth having for, and carving one exception for tidiness is how it stops meaning anything — so the 115 unremovable assets and 17 locations stay and stop looking like register data instead. The name is the right field because it is what every list view, every report and the PUBLIC homepage dropdown already renders. **`is_test_fixture` is derived and certain; the name mark is applied by matching a name convention and is only as good as that convention was.** Two mechanisms, two meanings, neither pretending to be the other. · Handoff 26 D.
- 2026-09-15 · `[ruled]` **The three unclassified tenants get a proposal with evidence; the owner marks.** `nrs19` → `client` (11,942 assets, 11,386 employees from a real NRS staff extract in `docs/seed/NRS_all_csvs/`, and the tenant Vendra's INT-03 work measures against). `cadra19` → `client` (1,335 assets seeded from real NRS contractor documents — Baron drawings, the Unecon M&E schedule, the FIRS HQ furniture inventory — describing a real building). `gtb19` → `test` (156 synthetic assets from a seed script, synthetic employees, 122 branch coordinates from public OpenStreetMap data, already carrying pytest fixtures; the live GTB tenant is `gtb`, which `k8s/dev/60-ingress.yaml:82` records was renamed from it). **The rule where it is genuinely ambiguous is `client`** — a test database wrongly marked `client` costs an afternoon; a client database wrongly marked `test` costs a register. Evidence per tenant in `docs/GO-LIVE.md` §7. · Handoff 26 C.

## 2026-09-16 — close-out (the last three rulings)

- 2026-09-16 · `[ruled]` **All three unclassified tenants are marked, including the safe one: `nrs19` → `client`, `cadra19` → `client`, `gtb19` → `test`.** **This supersedes TWO 2026-09-15 entries**, not one: the handoff-26 entry that left the marking to the owner's judgement, and the handoff-25 entry stating the three were *"deliberately left unmarked — fail-closed does the work, and marking a database `client` on a guess would be a false record"*. That reasoning was right when it was written and is now spent: the marks are no longer a guess, they rest on per-tenant provenance evidence, so the false-record objection falls away. Handoff 26's entry that *"all three of `cadra19`, `nrs19` and `gtb19` are unmarked"* also stops being true the moment the owner runs the commands, and it is load-bearing for that entry's argument about why both scripts refuse an unclassified database — the argument survives, the example does not. the evidence in `docs/GO-LIVE.md` §7 was accepted as sufficient and each tenant is now ruled rather than proposed. **`nrs19`** is agreed without reservation — a real NRS staff extract naming serving officials, and the register another organisation measures against. **`cadra19`** is decided by PROVENANCE, not by use: assets derived from a named architect's drawings, a named consultancy's M&E completion schedule and a dated FIRS HQ furniture inventory, describing a real 19-floor building, are client-derived content whatever the tenant is used for — and the argument that settles it is that `test19` already exists, is marked, and has a credential pair, so **a second test tenant is a thing to make, not a thing to reclassify**. **`gtb19`** is the one place *"when ambiguous, client"* is deliberately not applied, because it is not ambiguous: every category of its content traces to a source and none is client-supplied. **Marking the safe one matters as much as marking the other two** — unmarked refuses, so nothing is unsafe either way, but a database whose class is recorded only in a report is the exact shape of thing this programme spent three handoffs fixing. The caution about `gtb19` naming a DIFFERENT database on the DOKS cluster in `deploy-19.yml` and `tenant-cutover-19.yml` is carried with the mark, not resolved by it. · Close-out §1.
- 2026-09-16 · **Marking the three tenants weakens a guard in every case, and NOT the same guard — a claim the close-out did not make and a first draft got backwards.** The draft said `gtb19` → `test` was the only one of the three that weakens anything, reasoning that `client` and absent both refuse. **True of the test suites, false of the two owner-run scripts**, which gate on `db_class not in (TEST, CLIENT)` (`remove_test_residue.py:164`, `mark_test_fixtures.py:116`) — `client` is allowed on purpose, because `jaizbankplc` is marked `client` and is the register they were written for. So `gtb19` → `test` opens that database to the TEST SUITE, while `nrs19` → `client` and `cadra19` → `client` open theirs to the DELETE SCRIPT: once `nrs19` is marked, `remove_test_residue.py` under `CADRA_APPLY=1` will delete fixture-named rows from the database holding a real NRS staff extract and 11,942 assets — and **that script's own comment names `nrs19` as the reason its absent-refusal exists.** The error was in the permissive direction, telling the owner the two client-derived marks were consequence-free when they are the more consequential two. The ruling is unaffected and marking is still right; what needs the care is the delete script, not the marks. **Found by a verification agent reading the scripts, not by the author re-reading the prose** — the close-out's own closing lesson, demonstrated inside the close-out. `docs/GO-LIVE.md` §7. · Close-out, verification pass.
- 2026-09-16 · `[ruled]` **The quick-submit actor defect is fixed by SPLITTING the two paths, not by choosing one behaviour for both.** On the authenticated route, `.sudo()` is correct — it elevates privileges *and keeps the actor*, which is the entire difference between the two calls and the reason the actor is being lost. On the `auth='public'` twin there is no actor to keep, so `with_user(SUPERUSER_ID)` stays exactly as it is and **the public path does not change at all**. Handoff 26 weighed three options and **none was taken whole**: splitting applies its option 1 (`.sudo()`) to the authenticated route and its option 2 (leave it) to the public one, which is the only arrangement that fixes the defect **without touching unauthenticated behaviour** — answering the objection that blocked the change rather than covering it with a test. Not folded into the §9.1 pre-envelope refactor: that refactor is a prerequisite for nothing here. Recorded as a ruling so whoever picks it up implements it and nobody re-decides it. Handoff 26 was right not to change it inside a handoff about labelling; this is a pre-existing audit-trail defect it exposed rather than introduced. `docs/GO-LIVE.md` §11.1. · Close-out §2.
- 2026-09-16 · **CORRECTED: the two cutover credentials are a switched-off integration awaiting a credential, NOT a security item — and the two go to different people.** *"Two unrotated credentials in git history"* had been in the Architect's own status reporting for weeks and was **wrong in the direction that lets something sit.** Neither `myservice_identity.redhubs_api_key` nor `myservice_identity.sap_webhook_secret` is set on any of the eight local tenants, and both routes have refused since handoff 17 — so there is no live compromised credential. What is actually true is that **Redhubs' asset-register export and SAP's inbound employee sync have been answering `Unauthorized` since then, and two external parties are waiting on values somebody has to generate and hand over.** Filed as a security item it stays unactioned, because security items and stalled integrations are worked by different people. `docs/GO-LIVE.md` §8.3, whose phrasing stands as written. · Close-out §3, from report 26's fourth correction.
- 2026-09-16 · **ACCEPTED as they stand, not to be reopened:** §10.2's eleven remaining silent-instrument sites (a deliberate stopping point, recorded with file and line); §12's three judged-and-left findings; the 115 assets and 17 locations **marked rather than removed**, because append-only is the property the custody chain is worth having; the 460 unidentifiable service requests, permanent and stated as permanent with the date the count was taken, where inventing a fix would be worse; and the two honest limits on the register — what was known before handoff 19 and never written down, and §10.2. **A register that names its own edges is usable; one that claims completeness is not.** · Close-out §4.
- 2026-09-16 · **The two findings worth carrying out of this programme, both about verification rather than care.** First: `scripts/remove_test_residue.py` read its production refusal — **the one guard between `CADRA_APPLY=1` and a real client's register** — through an ormcache, using the exact call the handoff that wrote the script had just finished proving unsafe, three directories from the docstring explaining why. Nobody re-read the new file against the rule the same handoff had written down. Second: report 25's own correction introduced a fresh false claim about the seventeen locations, caught only by checking it. Fourteen models carried no self-label and one carried one; twenty-four silent instruments were found where a fourth was expected — **both numbers larger than anyone guessed, and both found by enumerating rather than by reasoning.** The lesson the programme ends on: *the thing that works is not care; it is somebody who did not write it reading it against the database.* · Close-out.

## 2026-09-16 — the asset list order

- 2026-09-16 · `[landed]` **`nrs.asset._order` is `asset_code desc`, and the `desc` is chosen over `create_date desc` rather than being the obvious spelling of it.** The register sorted by `asset_code` ascending. `asset_code` is a zero-padded `ir.sequence` (`NRS-AST-000001`), so ascending code order IS ascending creation order, and an asset a user had just created was placed deterministically on the LAST page — the single record they were looking for, put as far from them as the dataset allows. Because `_order` is the model default it reached list, kanban and every other view at once, and one clause fixes all of them: neither view overrode it (`asset_views.xml:227`, `:258`) and the action set no `default_order` (`:344`). **Descending the code sequence buys newest-first AND keeps the register ordered by code; `create_date desc` would have bought the first and quietly lost the second**, which is why `test_the_order_is_the_code_sequence_not_the_row_id` creates its rows with codes deliberately out of insertion order — it fails for `create_date desc` and for `id desc` and passes only for the code-ordered clause, so a later tidy-up cannot swap one for the other without noticing the cost.
- 2026-09-16 · **This is NOT an API change and Vendra cannot observe it.** `paginate()` sorts on `id` explicitly (`myservice_api/controllers/utils.py:967`) rather than falling back to `_order`, so `GET /api/v1/assets` and its cursor are untouched. Checked before the change rather than asserted after it, because a model default that the API silently inherited would have made this a breaking change to a contract another system builds against. If `paginate()` ever stops passing `order=`, this clause becomes API surface.
- 2026-09-16 · **The one way the fix could have failed was checked against the estate, not reasoned about.** Descending a code sequence only yields newest-first if the codes share a prefix — a tenant holding both `Jaiz-AST-…` and a legacy `LEGACY-…` would have pinned the legacy block permanently above every new asset, and the fix would have looked correct on the tenant it was tested on. Every tenant was counted: `jaizbankplc` 4,164 rows all `Jaiz-AST-`, `nrs19` 11,943 all `NRS-AST-`, `lasg` 150 all `LASG-AST-`, `gtb` 150 all `GTB-AST-` — **16,407 real assets, one prefix each, no mixed-prefix tenant in the estate.** Verified on `jaizbankplc` that the top of the default list is now the same record `create_date desc` picks.
- 2026-09-16 · **No migration, no schema change, and therefore no per-tenant fan-out.** `_order` is a pure-Python class attribute with no database representation, so the multi-tenant obligation in `CLAUDE.md` — which exists for STORED FIELDS, where an un-upgraded tenant raises `UndefinedColumn` — does not apply. One registry serves every database, so restarting the container made it live on all eight at once. The manifest version is deliberately NOT bumped: every bump in this module has a `migrations/` folder behind it, and a version with no migration implies one that does not exist.
- 2026-09-16 · **A survey of `_order` across every first-party model found `nrs.asset` to be the outlier rather than an instance of a pattern.** Everything created often already sorts newest-first — `request.request` (`id desc`), `nrs.asset.transfer` (`transfer_date desc`), tag labels, import jobs, SAP sync logs and dispatch logs (`create_date desc`), incidents and access logs (`date desc`). The only models with no `_order` at all are `nrs.asset.import.line` (child rows, where file order is what a reader wants and `id` gives it), `bus.fuel.budget` and `workflow.config`. Nothing else needed changing, so nothing else was changed.

## 2026-09-16 — the register the user actually looks at

- 2026-09-16 · `[landed]` **The asset-order fix was correct and landed on the wrong screen; `/app/assets/list` is the React SPA, not an Odoo view.** `nrs.asset._order` reaches Odoo's own list and kanban. The Cadra web register is an API CONSUMER — `dataProvider.fetchAssetsPage` calls `GET /api/v1/assets` — and `paginate()` passes `order="id"` explicitly rather than falling back to `_order`, so that screen kept showing oldest-first and a Vendra-created asset still landed on the last page. **The same fact was verified, reported and mis-read in one move:** "the API is unaffected, Vendra cannot observe it" was true, was the right thing to check, and was checked as a SAFETY property for the partner integration — nobody turned it around and asked which UI the complaint was about. A property that protects one consumer is not automatically good news for another; the question the check should have raised is *who else reads this endpoint*, and the answer was "the screen in the complaint".
- 2026-09-16 · `[ruled]` **`?sort` is an opt-in parameter, not a flipped default.** `GET /api/v1/assets?sort=newest|oldest`, absent meaning `oldest` — the order this endpoint has always published. Vendra builds against this contract, and CLAUDE.md rule 1 makes changing a published ordering a breaking change while an addition is safe; the register UI passes `sort=newest` and every other consumer sees nothing. Flipping the default to newest-first is arguably the better default for a register and is deliberately NOT done here — it is a versioned change that needs Vendra told first, and it stays available as a later decision rather than a side effect of a UI fix.
- 2026-09-16 · `[landed]` **The direction lives in the CURSOR, and a mismatch is a 400.** A keyset cursor means "everything after the last row I saw", and which side that is depends on the direction — so an ascending cursor replayed against a descending walk makes the comparator page AWAY from the unseen rows and return a short list that looks complete. That is the exact shape handoff 22 refused for corrupt cursors, so `paginate()` records `desc: True` in the descending payload and refuses a token whose direction disagrees with the request, carrying `cursor_direction` and `requested_direction`. **The ascending payload is unchanged** — `desc` is written only on the descending path — so every cursor already in an integration's hands keeps working, and a test asserts the ascending token still decodes to exactly `{"last_id": N}` rather than trusting that it does.
- 2026-09-16 · **A guard written for the new parameter was wrong on its first page and the test caught it, not the reasoning.** The direction check fired whenever `bool(cursor_data.get("desc")) != bool(descending)`, which refuses the FIRST page of a descending walk — there is no cursor there, so the check compared an absent direction against a requested one and called it a mismatch. It is now gated on a cursor actually having been sent. Worth recording because the bug was in the half of the change whose entire purpose is to stop a silent wrong answer, and the version that shipped it would have made `sort=newest` return a 400 every time.
- 2026-09-16 · `[landed]` **The register gets a visible Newest/Oldest control, because the page already looked sortable and was not.** `AssetRegister` renders column headers with sort icons driven by TanStack `getSortedRowModel`, which reorders only the rows already fetched — with a page size of 200 and infinite scroll it cannot reach a row the walk has not loaded, so on a register of any size those headers cannot surface the newest asset and give no sign of it. The new control is register-wide and served by the API. It is deliberately NOT part of `hasFilters`/`clearFilters`: clearing filters should not silently reorder the list. **`sortOrder` is in the react-query key**, which is load-bearing rather than tidy — a changed key starts a fresh query with no cursor, which is exactly the restart the cursor contract above requires; leaving it out would send the held cursor into the opposite walk and 400 the page.

## 2026-09-17 — the guard that agreed with itself only locally

- 2026-09-17 · `[landed]` **A bare `<file>.py::<name>` reference resolves only against the files the guard governs, because globbing the working tree made the answer depend on files that are not in the repository.** `test_a_comment_that_names_a_test_names_one_that_exists` passed on every developer's machine and failed in CI **on the same commit** — red on `dev` since PR #73, before any of this week's work. The resolver globbed the tree for a basename and resolved when exactly one file survived its exclusions: locally the untracked `_upgrade/` scratch tree holds a second `ir_actions.py`, so two matches meant "ambiguous, skip" and the test passed; a clean checkout has no `_upgrade/`, one match survived — `crnd-19/`, a **vendored** tree — it resolved, and `IrActionsServer` is not defined there. **An untracked directory was load-bearing in a guard about references.** The universe is now `_python_files_to_scan()`, which fixes both halves at once: the answer no longer varies with what happens to be lying in a working tree, and a reference can no longer be checked against a tree we do not control. An EXPLICIT path still resolves wherever it points — that is a statement about a specific file rather than a guess, and a miss there is still a real breakage.
- 2026-09-17 · **The fix was proved by cloning both states, which is the only way this class of bug is visible at all.** Running the test in the working tree — the obvious check — is exactly the thing that cannot see it, because the working tree is the condition that hides it. A clean clone of `dev` reproduces CI's failure locally in under a second; a clean clone of the branch passes. Recorded because the close-out ended on *"the thing that works is not care; it is somebody who did not write it reading it against the database"* — and this is the same sentence with the environment in place of the database: **a guard is only as trustworthy as the conditions it has been run under, and "it passes for me" names a condition rather than a result.**
- 2026-09-17 · `[landed]` **The resolver gets its own mutation proof, because the previous proof covered the matcher and not the thing that broke.** `test_a_bare_reference_resolves_only_against_files_this_guard_governs` asserts the universe excludes vendored and scratch trees, that `ir_actions.py` resolves to None, that a governed file still resolves by bare name — and that a missing name is still CAUGHT. Without that fourth assertion the first three are satisfied by a resolver that returns None for everything, which is the failure mode a restriction invites: narrowing a guard until it is quiet and calling that a pass.

## 2026-09-20 — handoff 27 (the cycle reopens: what has happened to this asset)

- 2026-09-20 · `[landed]` **The asset history is assembled by the API, not stitched together in the browser.** The Architect's ruling, for three reasons of which the third decides it: ordering across sources is the substance and an ordering nobody tests will be wrong; the permission and record-cap rules are settled server-side; and INT-10's external auditor asks the question NRS asked in the room and must get the same answer from the API. `GET /api/v1/assets/<id>/history`, built as `nrs.asset.asset_history()` in `myservice_api/models/asset_history.py` so the ordering can be asserted in-registry with the clock frozen. **The first half of that ruling was vindicated inside the handoff that landed it:** the ordering was wrong — anything in the same second as the registration sorted before it, because `create_date` carries microseconds and a custody link's `changed_at` does not — and 32 passing tests had not caught it. It was caught by opening the screen.
- 2026-09-20 · **Every event carries `evidence`, because the records are not worth the same and the list would otherwise say they were.** Measured in §A: a change of custodian is the only event whose record nobody can alter. A location move has a row only via `POST /move` or a completed transfer, and that row is editable and deletable by any asset manager and cascades away with the asset; every other writer of `location_id` leaves chatter alone (`nrs19`: 1,146 tracked location changes, 1 transfer row). Status, condition, verification and the disposal paperwork are chatter only. Five grades, defined in the response by what somebody can DO to the record — `append_only`, `system_stamp`, `guarded_record`, `editable_record`, `chatter` — because "durable" means whatever the reader hopes. The handoff's four requirements are one requirement, *do not imply more than the record supports*, and an ungraded list fails it on every line.
- 2026-09-20 · **Chatter IS a source, graded as what it is — not left out because it is weak.** The handoff says a `mail.message` is not evidence. It is also the ONLY record of a status change, a verification, and most location moves, all of which NRS watched. Omitting them would make the screen look cleaner and answer the client's question worse; naming them `chatter` on their face, with the grade's meaning one hover away and the count in `summary.statement`, is the honest form. Tracked VALUES only: message bodies are never published, because a note typed on an asset was not promised to every holder of `assets:read`.
- 2026-09-20 · **One physical move is one line, and a failed join is two lines rather than a wrong claim.** `POST /move` leaves a transfer row AND the chatter row of the `location_id` write it caused. They are joined when they name the same from/to pair within 120 s of the row's creation or last write, each side used once, closest first. The joined event takes its instant and actor from the chatter row (the write itself) and its paperwork from the transfer. Nothing is ever DROPPED on the strength of the join — an unmatched transfer and an unmatched chatter row both still appear.
- 2026-09-20 · **An actor the record does not have is null, including where a `create_uid` was sitting right there.** A transfer row with no chatter row has `actor: null`: `create_uid` is whoever RAISED the transfer, and the record does not say who moved the asset. A custody link with no `changed_by` is null, not "system". A chatter row's actor is its `author_id` resolved to a user, never its `create_uid` — migration 19.0.1.12.0 documents why the author beats the database user. (That migration itself falls back to the message's `create_uid` where a message had no author, so a RECONSTRUCTED link's actor can be one; such a link already says `reconstructed` on its face.) The front end prints *"No person recorded"*. The existing `RequestDetail.tsx` idiom of `?? "System"` was deliberately not copied, and is itself noted in GO-LIVE §13.3 item 9.
- 2026-09-20 · **Better records supersede their own chatter rows; nothing else is deduplicated.** `custodian_id` tracking is dropped because the custody chain was backfilled from those very rows and is a superset. The tag state fields are dropped in favour of the label's own stamps, which hold the same fact with a real actor. **The disposal approval's two columns were dropped the same way in the first draft, and that was wrong** — see the next entry. `location_id` is NOT in that list — it goes through the join, because most location changes have no better record.
- 2026-09-20 · **The disposal approval is `editable_record`, and a rewrite of the approver is listed — the verification pass's blocking finding, against the Builder.** The first draft graded it `guarded_record` and suppressed its tracking rows as "superseded by the guarded columns". They are not guarded: no `write` refuses them and both are inputs on the Odoo form; their absence from `_WRITABLE_FIELDS` is a fact about one door. So an asset manager could have changed who approved a disposal and the history would have shown the new name, graded as protected, with the only sign of the edit hidden — the exact overclaim the handoff forbids, committed in the module written to prevent it, on the strength of a research summary's word "guarded" that nobody checked against the model. Now only the row of the FIRST stamping is dropped (the same fact twice), every later change is a `disposal_recorded` event carrying old and new, and `test_a_rewritten_disposal_approver_is_visible` holds it.
- 2026-09-20 · **[ITS STATED REASON IS FALSE — corrected 2026-09-21, below and in GO-LIVE §14.1. The decision itself under-publishes and is safe.]** **A request's reference is not published on `assets:read`.** `request_count` already tells every holder of that scope that requests exist; the request itself belongs to `requests:read`, and a route cannot see its caller's scopes today. The event says a request was raised, when and by whom. What else the history newly exposes to `assets:read` — the names of the internal users who made each tracked change, a transfer's recorder and approver, outbox references — is listed in report 27 §6 for a ruling.
- 2026-09-20 · **Sorted to the whole second, with a fixed rank inside it.** Second-precision and microsecond-precision timestamps cannot be compared raw (above). Inside one second the sources cannot say what came first, so `_RANK` does, in the only order those things can have happened in: the registration, then the tag minted with it, then everything else. The test that pins it was proved to fail with the fix disabled (`'custody_changed' != 'registered'`) before it was trusted.
- 2026-09-20 · **Newest first, on the screen and as the API's default.** The reader's first question about an asset is where it is now and how it got there. `?sort=oldest` reads it forwards, and is the exact reverse — asserted. This is the opposite default from `GET /assets`, whose `oldest` predates `?sort`; here there was no older behaviour to keep. Not paged and not capped: a merge over seven sources has no single id for a cursor to walk, and `CAP_EXEMPT_REASON_CUSTODY` applies to it word for word.
- 2026-09-20 · **"Nothing after registration" does not count the tag minted with the asset.** Every `create` mints a tag in the same transaction, so without this no asset would ever have had an empty history and *"There is no recorded history for this asset"* — the sentence the handoff exists for — could never have been reached on the screen it was written for.
- 2026-09-20 · **The statements are plain words, because they are shown to a client.** The first draft named API fields in backticks and printed a microsecond timestamp; the front end was stripping the backticks. The server owns the words, so the server was fixed and the stripping helper deleted. `test_the_statements_are_words_not_field_names` holds it.
- 2026-09-20 · **NOT built: an append-only record of location moves, and the approver NRS asked about.** The first is the real fix for the largest finding in §A and has the custody recorder's shape — but it is a stored model and a fan-out across every tenant, and the handoff's instruction was *"an ordering and a merge, not a new record"*. The second is §D: a process question for NRS and the owner. Both are in `docs/GO-LIVE.md` §13 with the facts, and §13.2 says why the first must precede the second.
- 2026-09-20 · **The Move dialog said "Creates an immutable transfer record". It does not, and it said so on the screen NRS watched.** Corrected to say what the record is. The custody dialog said its audit trail was `mail.thread`; since handoff 14 it is the append-only chain, which is the stronger and the true claim. Found by the front-end pass, fixed because a history that grades a move `editable_record` beside a dialog that calls it immutable is the product disagreeing with itself in front of a client.

## 2026-09-20 — handoff 28 (where a thing has been)

- 2026-09-20 · `[landed]` **Location gets the custody treatment: `nrs.asset.movement`, append-only, written in `nrs.asset.write` and `nrs.asset.create` where every writer passes.** The Architect's ruling on report 27's Q1. A register that can prove who held a laptop and not where it was has a hole in the shape of the question people actually ask; and a transfer row was worse than nothing in one way — it LOOKS like a record, and it is editable, deletable, cascade-deleted with the asset, holds a date and has no field for who moved the thing. Same guards as custody and for its reasons: `create` refuses without the recorder's flag, `write` and `unlink` raise unconditionally (so they hold under `sudo()`), the ACLs are read-only for all three groups, `asset_id` is `ondelete='restrict'`, `movement_ids` is `copy=False`. A stored model, so a fan-out: all eight local tenants upgraded one at a time and each verified in its own database.
- 2026-09-20 · `[landed]` **`PATCH` keeps accepting `location_id`, and does NOT write an `nrs.asset.transfer`.** Q2 answered itself once the recorder was at the model layer. Blocking the quiet door is the instinct handoff 14 punished; recording underneath every door makes it stop being quiet without anybody having to find it. And the two records are different things — the movement is the FACT that the asset moved, the transfer is a PROCESS DOCUMENT with a draft, an approver and a done. A move is not always a transfer, so `transfer_id` is set where one exists (`/move`, a completed transfer) and empty where it does not, and nothing invents one. `transfer_reference` is recorded WITH the move because the document is deletable and the move must outlive its paperwork.
- 2026-09-20 · **The writers were searched for, not taken from the handoff — and the one that mattered was not in the Python.** `nrs_asset.location_id` is `ON DELETE SET NULL` and `generic_location.parent_id` is `ON DELETE CASCADE`: deleting a building empties the location of every asset in every room under it, in one statement, with no `write` and not even a tracking row. It was found by reading the foreign key (`pg_constraint`), and is closed by `generic.location.unlink` recording each doomed asset — `child_of`, because the cascade never calls `unlink` on the children — as a move to nowhere with `source = 'location_deleted'`, BEFORE the delete, so a refused delete rolls the rows back with it.
- 2026-09-20 · **The Odoo form had never been able to save an asset's location.** `location_id` is `readonly` on the form and filled by the room onchange; Odoo's web client drops a readonly field from the save unless it carries `force_save` (`record.js`, `_getChanges`, read in the 19 image). So picking a room showed a new Location, stored the room, and threw the location away — and the handoff's own writer list, and the first draft of this handoff's docstrings, named "the Odoo form" as a writer. It was not one. Measured harmless: three of four tenants never use building/floor/room and on `nrs` all 1,272 agree. `force_save="1"` makes the form store what it shows, and the recorder then sees it.
- 2026-09-20 · **The importer no longer moves an asset to nowhere on a blank cell.** On an UPDATE line `vals` always carried `location_id`, so a blank cell — or a name with a typo, which `ilike` resolves to nothing — wrote `False`. Already wrong; with an append-only recorder underneath it would also have been PERMANENT. A file that does not say where an asset is has not said it moved. The line now says *"matched no location, so the asset was left at …"*.
- 2026-09-20 · **No backfill from chatter, and the measurement says that was the right ruling before it was asked.** Of `nrs19`'s 1,146 tracked location changes, 1,145 were written in six seconds on the day the register was loaded — a script re-mapping locations onto a hierarchy. A backfill would have minted 1,145 permanent movement records of assets that never moved, in a table that refuses correction. `tracking_backfill` exists in the `source` selection and NOTHING writes it: the property *a reconstruction says so* has to exist at the source before any reconstruction does, so that a backfill ruled in later has a label waiting rather than a reason to borrow one.
- 2026-09-20 · **Report 27's headline number was not looked inside, by the Builder.** *"1,146 tracked moves, 1 transfer row"* was a true count offered as the scale of unrecorded moves. It is one six-second script. The structural finding is proven by test and stands; the number is a seed artefact, and it was caught only because handoff 28 asked what a backfill would recover. **A count is a claim about what the rows ARE, and that was never checked.** Corrected in INT-10 §2.4, GO-LIVE §13.2 and §13.4, and at the top of report 28.
- 2026-09-20 · `[landed]` **The Verify button records a verifier: `POST /assets/<id>/verify`.** Takes no date and no verifier — today, and the acting user, for `approve-disposal`'s reason: a verifier a client can name is a claim. `last_verified_by` is published and stays out of `_WRITABLE_FIELDS`. `PATCH` of the date is NOT closed; it is how an integration carries a verification made elsewhere, and what it cannot do is say who. **What it buys, plainly:** one overwritten column and a chatter line. It is not an append-only record of verifications.
- 2026-09-20 · **A verification date that arrives without a verifier CLEARS the verifier — the verification pass's blocking finding, against the Builder, and the same shape as report 27's.** `last_verified_date` is PATCH-writable; `last_verified_by` is not. The first draft left it at that, so a PATCH of the date kept the PREVIOUS verifier beside it: verified by Alice in January, PATCHed with a June date, and the asset read *"verified in June, by Alice"*, who checked nothing in June — naming a person the record does not have, in the handoff whose ruling was that a verification without a person is a date. Three published sentences already said it could not happen, and the test written to hold them PATCHed a never-verified asset, so its null was the starting state and proved nothing. Fixed in `nrs.asset.write`, not in the route, because the date has more than one writer; the test now verifies FIRST.
- 2026-09-20 · **`last_verified_by` IS tracked — reversing the Builder's first decision.** The first draft left it untracked on the grounds that the verifier is already the author of the `verified` event. That is true only when the DATE changes. Two verifications on one day change no date: the asset says Bob and the only line in its history says Alice. So it is tracked, mapped to `verified`, and joins the six provenance fields in `SNAPSHOT_TRACKED_FROM` — which also PUBLISHES it on the snapshot for the first time (it was `not_recoverable`). On the eight local databases the stamp predates this field's tracking by about 3½ hours, because it was added in a second pass; on any deployment upgraded once, both land together. A verification's `notes` now describe that verification: a note-less one clears them rather than leaving the previous verifier's words under the new name.
- 2026-09-20 · **A fresh install is stamped too, so an absent stamp never has to mean anything.** The first draft read absence as "tracked since the first asset", reasoning that a fresh install runs no migration. True of a database whose every asset this code created — and false of the one that matters: the 15→19 cutover builds NEW databases and loads them by ETL with their original `create_date`s, so the snapshot would have rolled a GRN reference back across years it holds no record of. `post_init_hook` stamps at install. (It also made a test true in CI that had been true only locally: CI builds a fresh database, had no stamp, and the test asserted one.)
- 2026-09-20 · **A movement joins its own chatter row by TRANSACTION, not by the clock; and a caller cannot borrow an approval or choose a label.** Three findings of the verification pass. (1) The join matched on two wall-clock instants within 120 s; a message's `date` is taken at precommit, so in any import longer than two minutes every early move came apart from its own chatter row and was listed twice. Both rows share Postgres's transaction stamp, so they join on `create_date`, and by recorded NAME where a deleted location has nulled the id. (2) `cadra_movement_transfer_id` arrives in the context, which a caller controls, and the history shows the linked document's APPROVER inside an append-only event: a transfer is now linked only if it is this asset's and names this destination. NRS have asked for approvals on moves; that was the forgery path to one. The transfer block also now says in-band that it is `editable_record`. (3) `source` was claimable through the context, so anybody able to move an asset could file the move under `test`; only the four route-level sources may be claimed. Custody has the same hole and was not touched — GO-LIVE §13.4 item 0.
- 2026-09-20 · **`generic.location.unlink` records inside a savepoint.** The rows are written before `super().unlink()` has checked anything, and a refusal that raises in Python does not abort the cursor — so a caller that caught it would have kept permanent rows saying a location was deleted that is still there.
- 2026-09-20 · `[landed]` **The six provenance fields are tracked — and that was not "one line per field, no migration".** Two guards in `test_snapshot_rollback.py` pull in opposite directions by design: a tracked field MUST be in `SNAPSHOT_ROLLED_BACK`, and MUST NOT be in `SNAPSHOT_AS_OF_TODAY`. So tracking them forced six fields across the boundary of a published report, where the report says of a rolled-back field that *no recorded change since the date means the current value IS the value at the date*. For a date before tracking began that is false. So migration `19.0.1.15.0` stamps `cadra.provenance_tracked_from` on each database ONCE — measured at the upgrade, not typed into the source, where it would be wrong on every server upgraded later — and `snapshot_fidelity(as_of)` classes the six `as_of_today` for an earlier date and `rolled_back` from it on. Eight tenants, eight different stamps.
- 2026-09-20 · **That disclosure is said once per report, not on every row — an existing test made that decision, against the Builder's first draft.** The first draft flagged each row under `_fidelity.as_of_today`. `test_fidelity_keys_are_absent_when_the_row_is_sound` failed it, rightly: `_fidelity` on a row is a signal only because a sound row does not carry one, and on a database whose tracking began today the flag would have sat on every row of every historical snapshot, where every reader learns to skip it. It is a fact about the DATE asked for, so it lives in the classification that moves with the date.
- 2026-09-20 · **Tracking buys VISIBILITY, not protection, and the provenance fields probably want more.** A changed GRN reference is now a `provenance_changed` event graded `chatter`: visible, which it was not, and deletable by the same people who can make it. The Builder's view, offered for a ruling and NOT acted on: `grn_reference`, `purchase_order_reference` and `sap_asset_number` are the three an auditor reconciles against Vendra and SAP, they change rarely, and a change to one is exactly the event somebody would want to hide. They are better candidates for the §A treatment than verification is. Cost — which the handoff named among them — was not tracked: it is not one of the six, and money fields are a separate ruling with `value` and depreciation attached.
- 2026-09-20 · **The opening state is not history.** The `create` that makes an asset also mints its tag and — when it is created at a location or already assigned — writes its first movement and first custody link, in one transaction and so under one `create_date`. Counting those as things that happened AFTER registration would mean no asset created at a location ever had an empty history, and the sentence handoff 27 exists for could never be reached. They are listed and not counted. This CHANGES handoff 27's behaviour for an asset created already assigned, whose first custody link used to count; it is the same rule applied evenly. The rule's edge: a script that creates an asset and then moves it in ONE transaction has that first move read as opening state — which understates, never overclaims.
- 2026-09-20 · **A refusal happens before anything is written — learned by breaking it.** The first draft validated `/move`'s `reason` AFTER creating the transfer document, so a refused request answered 400 and left a transfer in state `done` for a move that never happened: a false record, written by the change meant to end them. An error RESPONSE does not roll a transaction back; only an exception does. Found by fact-checking the endpoint reference — which documented a `422 unprocessable` this route has never returned — against a live response, and pinned by `test_a_refused_move_leaves_nothing_behind`.
- 2026-09-20 · **A published key was NOT renamed.** `fidelity.not_recorded`'s `provenance_change` became untrue as worded (a change IS now recorded). The first draft renamed it `provenance_change_before_tracking`; that is a breaking change to a response one handoff old, for nothing. The key stays and its sentence changed.
- 2026-09-20 · **A statement on a client's screen goes stale the day the behaviour under it changes, and nothing fails.** `fidelity.verification` said the Verify button *"sets that date and nothing else"* — written in handoff 27, made false by handoff 28, and caught by the front-end pass, not by a test. The Move dialog the same: "immutable" (false) → "can be edited or deleted" (true, then half the truth) → names both records. Both are now held by tests. The pattern is the finding: words the server owns need a test that knows what they claim.
- 2026-09-20 · `[landed]` **Three more screens.** *Legacy Data* printed *"Migrated from FIRS legacy system · March 2024"* on every asset, including ones Vendra registered this month; it is now *Provenance* and shows only what the record holds, or says in words that it holds nothing. `RequestDetail` printed *"System"* for a message with no author; it prints *No person recorded*. Evidence grades were hover-only; the label is now a button that opens a legend, reachable by tap and by keyboard, because NRS are shown this system on screens people touch.
- 2026-09-20 · **[FALSE — retracted 2026-09-21. `require_jwt` sets `request.jwt_claims`; see below and GO-LIVE §14.1.]** ~~`[landed]`~~ **`require_jwt` discards scopes — registered as an architectural fact (GO-LIVE §14), not a to-do.** It forecloses per-scope shaping anywhere in this API; where a response would cross a scope boundary the only tool is to remove the field for everybody.

## 2026-09-21 — handoff 29 (every control on the asset page)

- 2026-09-21 · **RETRACTION. `require_jwt` does NOT discard scopes, and "per-scope shaping is foreclosed anywhere in this API" was never true.** `utils.py`, last lines of `require_jwt`: `request.jwt_claims = claims`. The whole validated token is on the request, and three places already read it. Handoff 27's reviewer wrote the false statement in a summary; the Builder searched for three attribute names that do not exist and never opened the function; report 27 repeated it; the Architect ruled on it (handoff 28 §D, *"an architectural fact, not a to-do"*); the Builder registered it as one. Found in handoff 29 by reading `whoami` for an unrelated reason. The two 2026-09-20 entries that assert it are annotated in place, and GO-LIVE §14.1 is rewritten as the retraction. **What it changes:** the asset history withholds a maintenance request's reference from everybody because it supposedly could not be shown only to holders of `requests:read`. It can. Withholding under-publishes, so nothing was exposed; whether to publish it is a ruling, `[open]`. **The fifth instance, and the first about our own code promoted to a design constraint.**
- 2026-09-21 · `[landed]` **One status slot, not two pills.** The Architect left the shape open — a second pill, or one pill showing lifecycle when it is anything but active — and fixed the requirement: a missing, disposed or condemned asset must never render as cleared. **One slot:** `AssetStatusPills` renders the LIFECYCLE pill when `state` is not `active`, and the data-quality pill otherwise. *"Missing · Cleared"* side by side is a contradiction on its face, and a data-quality verdict about an asset nobody can find is not a fact anyone holds. Underneath, `deriveStatus` returns a new member, **"Not in service"**, for every non-active asset — so the register's *Cleared* filter and *Group by status* stop sweeping disposed assets into Cleared, which a rendering-only fix would have left. Proved by acting: `active → missing` in the row, *Missing* on the page and in the list, no step lit.
- 2026-09-21 · **The lifecycle LABEL is read from the model; only its COLOUR is declared.** `LifecyclePill` takes its words from `useEnumLabel("asset_state")` and keeps a colour map with a `?? UNKNOWN_STATE` fallback — `ConditionBadge`'s pattern — waived by name in `tests/test_frontend_enums.py`, which otherwise fails the build on any page declaring a model-owned set. The *Lifecycle State* row on the detail page printed the raw key (`in_repair`) two lines from rows that labelled `condition` and `domain` properly; it reads the label now.
- 2026-09-21 · **The three unreachable statuses were DEAD, not missing wiring. Deleted.** Measured: nothing in the back end models an allottee/nominal-roll check (`nominal` occurs only in prose about the employee import), a VIN on an asset (`vin_sn` is `fleet.vehicle`'s; the asset page's *"Serial / VIN"* row is `serial_number`), or duplicate detection (the SAP-number check RAISES, it does not flag). Gone from the type, the pill, the register's filter — where choosing one returned zero rows with no explanation — the dashboard's colours, the mock rows and the CSS (all three sets of custom properties; the first pass removed two and this entry said "the CSS"). The *Audit & Review* tab's "Possible Duplicate Detected" panel branched on one and is gone with it; its heading *"Approval Status"* is now *"Status"*, because no approval record exists in this system and the heading claimed one. **One real audit signal the front end never shows: `sap_reconciliation_state = exception`.** Not wired in handoff 29; it is the honest candidate for a second review status.
- 2026-09-21 · **The stepper no longer paints earlier steps green.** *Cleared → In Review → Disposed* marked every step BEFORE the current one as passed. Driving it from lifecycle puts a disposed asset on step 3 — and would therefore have drawn a green *Cleared* on every condemned asset, breaching the fixed requirement through the fix. Only the current step is drawn; a missing, under-repair or transferred asset lights none, because none of the three describes it. Caught by the front-end pass, not asked for.
- 2026-09-21 · `[landed]` **The browser's dialogs are gone, and *Mark missing* did NOT gain a reason.** Two `confirm()` calls in the whole app; both are app dialogs now, and a test fails if a native dialog fires. The Architect suspected *why is this missing* is an auditor's first question and said to check what the API accepts before adding it. It accepts nothing: `PATCH {"state": "missing"}`, and the model has no field FOR a reason — its three free-text fields (`notes`, `verification_notes`, `disposal_reason`) each mean something else. (This entry first said `notes` was the ONLY free text on an asset. It is one of three; the verification pass caught it, in five places.) Registered (GO-LIVE §13.5.1), not invented. The dialog's old text promised *"you can move/verify it again later"*; neither changes `state`. It now says what does: Edit.
- 2026-09-21 · **Condemn sends the condition too — and KEEPS its browser-clock date, against the Builder's own instruction.** Odoo's action of the same name writes `state = disposed` AND `condition = decommissioned`; the app's button wrote one, so a condemned asset read *Good* (all 140 disposed assets on `jaizbankplc`). Fixed. The Builder ALSO told the front-end pass to drop `disposal_date`, asserting the server stamps it — from a grep hit read without its context. It stamps it only inside `action_condemn`, which the API never calls; the pass refused, citing the repo's own test, and acting confirmed it: `disposal_date = None`. Dropping it would have put `no_disposal_date` on every disposal made from the app. The date stays, wrong for one hour a night, until there is a condemn route (GO-LIVE §13.5.2).
- 2026-09-21 · **Counts that fold missing and disposed assets into stock: fixed where the front end could, relabelled where it could not.** *Unverified* was `is_verified=false` with no state filter and *Verified* was `total − unverified`, so every disposed asset counted as verified: **on `nrs19` the app reported *Verified: 1*, and that one asset is the missing one.** Both are now over ACTIVE assets. The asset dashboard's strip gains a *Missing* tile in place of a permanently blank *PM Overdue* and is relabelled *On register*, so that it adds up for the first time (1,105 + 0 + 6 + 144 = 1,255 on `test19`, checked against the table). The home dashboard's *Review Queue* showed OPEN SERVICE-DESK TICKETS under *"Assets pending data quality review"*; it shows assets. Its *Approval Breakdown* donut plotted five hard-coded numbers as percentages of the live total; it plots three real ones. Left: every other *Total*, which needs the back end (§13.5.5).
- 2026-09-21 · **The persisted query cache was bumped `v1 → v2`, because the fix lives in the adapter and the cache holds what the adapter PRODUCED.** Restored objects are shown as stored (`refetchOnMount: false`, six-hour `maxAge`), so every browser that had opened the register was holding assets already stamped *Cleared*. Without the bump the fix ships and the green pill stays on screen, out of the user's own localStorage. The rule is now written beside the constant.
- 2026-09-21 · **Dead controls removed rather than left looking alive.** The notifications bell had no handler and a PERMANENT unread dot — a claim on every screen that something was waiting. The top bar printed *"Admin User / Administrator"* for every session, beside a Verify button that since handoff 28 writes the signed-in person into the register; it reads `GET /auth/whoami`, which had existed unused, and says *Signed in* rather than a name when it cannot. Four map-filter tabs set state nothing read. The review-queue card had `onClick={() => {}}`. *Back to Asset Register* went to the KPI dashboard.
- 2026-09-21 · `[landed]` **"Today" in the register snapshot is a Lagos day, as the cutoff already was.** The future-date check compared `as_of` with the UTC date, so for the first hour of every Lagos day a request for today's register was refused as *"a date that has not happened"*. Not this handoff's subject: it surfaced because the suite was run at 00:50 and nine tests went red — and CI, in UTC on both sides, can never see it. `snapshot_today()` beside `snapshot_cutoff()`; the same nine passed at 00:51, still inside the hour.
- 2026-09-21 · **`refetchOnMount` is `true`, app-wide — the verification pass's blocking finding, and not only about this handoff.** The app set `refetchOnMount: false` for "snappy navigation". `invalidateQueries` REFETCHES a query that is on screen and merely FLAGS one that is not; a flagged query that is never refetched on mount is shown from cache for ever. So with `false`, invalidating anything off screen did nothing — for EVERY mutation in the app, not only these. At the moment of any action on an asset the user is on its page, so the list and every count are off screen. **Acted:** mark an asset missing, open the dashboard from the sidebar — *Missing: 8* on screen, 9 in the database, same session. `true` refetches on mount only when the data is STALE, so a revisit inside `staleTime` is still instant, which is all `false` was chosen for. The Builder's first version fixed the pill and the keys and claimed the cure in a docstring; the acting harness never caught it because it loaded each page fresh, AFTER acting. An e2e spec now walks list → asset → act → dashboard in one page session.
- 2026-09-21 · **The Edit form never hydrates from a cached copy.** It sends `state` and `condition` on every save, so a stale copy is destructive: open Edit once, mark the asset missing, open Edit again — acted — and the State select read *Active*; saving any field would have written `active` back with a 200. And the new Mark-missing dialog SENDS people to Edit to reverse it. `gcTime: 0`, `refetchOnMount: "always"`, hydrate only from the fetch made after mount; `asset-raw` and `whoami` are never persisted to disk.
- 2026-09-21 · **The query cache is cleared on sign-IN as well as sign-out.** Clearing only on sign-out missed the ordinary end of a session on a shared PC — A's token expires with the tab closed, so no sign-out ever happens; B opens the app, A's persisted cache is restored, B signs in — and B reads A's cached data, the top bar's name included, beside a Verify button that records B. Worse than the hard-coded name it replaced, and introduced by the Builder in the act of replacing it.
- 2026-09-21 · **A fix for one review item broke every dialog, and only acting caught it.** "A dialog cannot be closed while its request is in flight" (so a refusal is never lost) was implemented as a guard in the close handler — which the SUCCESS handler also goes through, while `isPending` still reads true in its closure. Every dialog stayed open after a successful save. Typecheck clean, build clean; found on the first act. The success path now says it is the success path.
- 2026-09-21 · **Condemn's date is the browser's LOCAL calendar date.** `toISOString()` is the UTC date: yesterday for the first hour of a Lagos day, with a correct clock. Local removes that hour for a user in Lagos. Still not the server's clock — that needs the condemn route (GO-LIVE §13.5.2).
- 2026-09-21 · **A test that blessed a dead control was inverted.** `12-mock-pages.spec.ts` clicked the notifications bell, confirmed nothing crashed, and annotated it *"inert by design"*. It now asserts there is no such button and that the top bar names somebody. `04-assets-list`'s dropdown test had its casing fixed by the front-end pass but kept the `if (count)` guard that had let it prove nothing; the guard is gone.

## 2026-09-21 — an asset with no location can be placed (owner-reported)

- 2026-09-21 · `[landed]` **`POST /assets/<id>/move` PLACES an asset that has no location, and makes no transfer document for it.** The owner hit *"validation_failed: Required field missing: from_location_id"* moving an asset from the app. The route always built an `nrs.asset.transfer`, and that model requires both locations — so an asset with none could not be given one through Move at all. **Not an edge case: 358 of `nrs19`'s assets and 1,920 of `jaizbankplc`'s (46%) have no location.** Handoff 28 had already ruled what the answer is — the move is the FACT, the transfer is PAPERWORK, a move is not always a transfer — and its reviewer had flagged this exact case as "newly reachable"; it was registered and not fixed. The append-only movement records the placement (who, when, where, why, no `from`); `data.transfer` is `null`; `data.movement_id` moves to the top level because there it is the only record. The alternative — relaxing `from_location_id` on the transfer model — was rejected: a transfer FROM NOWHERE is a document saying something that did not happen, and it would have needed a migration on every tenant to say it.
- 2026-09-21 · **The app says *Place*, not *Move*, for such an asset, and its dialog promises no transfer.** The Move dialog told every user *"A transfer document is also created"*. For an unplaced asset that stopped being true the moment the route was fixed — so the button, the title, the confirm and the description all follow whether the asset has a location. Acted through the real dialog: `location_id NULL → 65`, 1 movement row, 0 transfer rows, history *"Placed at … · Cannot be altered"*, and the button turned back to *Move*.
- 2026-09-21 · **Why no test had caught it.** The pytest fixtures create every asset WITH a location; the e2e suite's `createAsset` creates every asset WITHOUT one, and its three move specs skip on `test19` for want of two *building* locations — so the one suite holding unplaced assets never moved one. Both now do.

## 2026-09-21 — handoff 30 (before the fourth twin)

- 2026-09-21 · `[ruled]` **Three more things get a record nobody can alter: every verification, every change of status with its reason, and a change to `grn_reference`, `purchase_order_reference` or `sap_asset_number`.** Not ruled, deliberately: cost and `value` — money carries depreciation with it and is a separate decision. `purchase_vendor` and the three import fields stay tracked in the message log and stay out: the importer rewrites them on every re-import, and an append-only table is the wrong place to discover that a record is mostly data loading (report 28 §0).
- 2026-09-21 · **They are ONE model, `nrs.asset.event`, not three more copies of custody — and custody and movement were left exactly where they are.** The Architect asked for the case before ruling; it is report 30 §1. *For* one model: custody was written on 9 September with a `source` any caller could claim through the context, movement was copied from it WITHOUT that hole, and the original kept it for twelve days — the copy was fixed and the original was not; and each pinned table is paid for in seven kinds of place. *Against* bringing the two live models in: a migration is a rewrite of the two tables whose whole value is that nobody rewrites them; it must preserve `create_date` or every auditor is told custody recording began this week; and the ids and the model names are PUBLISHED — `custody_id`, `movement_id`, every history event's `record` — and named in the INT-10 audit contract. **~~`[open]`~~ `[ruled yes, landed in handoff 31 — see below]` — whether custody and movement should adopt `nrs.asset.append.only.mixin`**, which defines no fields and therefore needs no migration and changes no id. Recommended; not done, because it changes two live audit models nobody has ruled on.
- 2026-09-21 · **An event is a CHANGE, so an asset's opening values write none** — a deliberate difference from custody and movement, which record the first custodian and the first placement. Every asset has a `state` from birth; recording it would pin every asset ever created (`ondelete='restrict'`) for a row that says only that it was created. The first change of a reference carries the opening value in `from_value`.
- 2026-09-21 · **"Ada checked it" and "a date was written" are two event TYPES, not one type with two `source`s.** The first draft used `source` to tell them apart, and the first real call came back `source = test` — the credential relabel overwrites it, as it does for custody. A distinction an auditor relies on must not be carried by a label something else is entitled to erase. `verified` is honoured only when the write also names the actor as verifier and dates it today, which is what `action_verify` does and what a caller claiming it through the context would have to do too — at which point the claim is true.
- 2026-09-21 · `[ruled]` **Disposed is terminal, and the rule is in `nrs.asset.write`.** A disposed asset cannot change `state`, `location_id` or `custodian_id`, through any door. Two doors were open, not one: the PATCH the handoff named, and `nrs.asset.transfer.action_complete`, which wrote `state: active` unconditionally and so un-disposed whatever it was completed on. **No context flag goes round it** — a flag is something an RPC caller can set. **And two doors `write` cannot see were shut after the verification pass found them:** `location_id` and `custodian_id` are both `ON DELETE SET NULL`, so deleting a location or an employee moved or un-assigned a disposed asset with no write at all. `generic.location.unlink` and a new `hr.employee.unlink` refuse, and offer archiving. The second also closes custody's twin of the hole handoff 28 closed for location: deleting an employee now appends the custody link the database used to make silently (`source: direct`, the reason says which — `source` is a published enum and was not widened for it). A write that NAMES the fields without changing them is allowed, because the app's edit form re-sends them on every save, and a disposal's paperwork is completed after it. `missing` is not terminal. The transition table is report 30 §2 and is the specification.
- 2026-09-21 · **Un-disposing is refused outright, on a measurement.** Across all eight local databases the tracked history holds ONE transition out of `disposed`: `NRS-AST-047641`, an `E2E-…` fixture, 19:37 on 20 September — handoff 29's own probe, the act that discovered the hole. Nobody has ever un-disposed a real asset. `[open]` — what a reversal should be if one is ever needed (the one case that can be constructed is condemning the wrong asset by mistake): report 30 §2 proposes a recorded act, by a manager, with a required reason, refused once the disposal is approved. Not built.
- 2026-09-21 · **`POST /assets/<id>/condemn` stamps a LAGOS day, not `context_today`.** The handoff said the route "kills the one-hour-a-night bug at its source". As specified it would have MOVED it: `context_today` resolves the acting user's timezone and falls back to UTC, and every API credential's user has none (read from `test19` and `nrs19`) — so every disposal made between 00:00 and 01:00 in Lagos would have been dated to the day before, by the server, where handoff 29's browser-side fix could not reach it. `action_condemn` now uses `snapshot_today()`: one definition of the day, the one the register snapshot cuts on.
- 2026-09-21 · **`reason` is REQUIRED on `/mark-missing` and `/condemn`.** On a new route, loosening later breaks nobody and tightening later would. `PATCH {"state": "missing"}` stays open and records no reason, as PATCH stayed open for location in handoff 28.
- 2026-09-21 · `[ruled]` **`building_id`, `floor_id` and `room_id` come out of `_WRITABLE_FIELDS`. BREAKING, announced.** The one live sender was our own edit form, which has no input for them: it hydrated them from the response and echoed them back on every save. Removing them without removing that echo would have raised a rejected-field warning on every asset edit, for three fields the user never touched — so the order the handoff set (surface rejections FIRST) had a third step it did not name.
- 2026-09-21 · ~~`[open]`~~ `[ruled, landed in handoff 31 — see below]` **400 or 200-with-a-warning for a field the API does not accept — recommendation in report 30 §3, not changed.** The app now shows `_warnings.rejected_fields` to the person who caused it.
